<A name="H79937105"></A>
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>ITEM 2. PROPERTIES</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">We operate a fleet of seven major construction vessels, one DSV, four MSVs, and one OSV. Our major
construction vessels currently include five barges and two ships. During 2010, construction was
completed on the first of our new pipelay/derrick vessels, the <B><I>Global 1200. </I></B>The <B><I>Global 1200</I></B>
recently completed its sea trials and was added to our fleet. All of our major construction
vessels are designed to perform more than one type of construction project, which reduces the risk
that these combination vessels will experience extended periods of idleness. A listing of our
major construction vessels along with a brief description of the capabilities of each is presented
on page&nbsp;24 of this Form 10-K. Our significant vessels and equipment are described below. In
addition, we are continuing the expansion of our fleet with the construction of the remaining
derrick/pipelay vessel, the <B><I>Global 1201</I></B>, with an expected delivery date in the third quarter of
2011.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">The <B><I>Hercules</I></B>, a major construction vessel, is a 486-foot dynamically positioned (&#147;DP&#148;)
pipelay/heavy-lift barge with a 2,000-ton crane capable of performing revolving lifts up to
approximately 1,600 tons. The <B><I>Hercules </I></B>can lay pipe up to 60 inches in diameter using the
conventional method and is currently assigned to our North America OCD segment in the U.S Gulf of
Mexico.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">The <B><I>Titan 2</I></B>, a major construction vessel, is a 408-foot self-propelled twin-hulled DP derrick ship
capable of lifting 882 tons. We lease the <B><I>Titan 2 </I></B>from a third party under a long-term charter
agreement. In February&nbsp;2011, we gave notice to the lessor of the <B><I>Titan 2 </I></B>of our intent to
terminate the lease effective in the second quarter of 2011. The current base of operations for
the <B><I>Titan 2 </I></B>is Mexico&#146;s Bay of Campeche.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">The <B><I>Comanche</I></B>, a major construction vessel, is a 401-foot pipelay derrick barge capable of lifts up
to approximately 1,000 tons. The <B><I>Comanche </I></B>can lay pipe up to 48 inches in diameter using the
conventional method and is currently assigned to our Asia Pacific/India segment.
</DIV>



<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">The <B><I>Iroquois</I></B>, a major construction vessel, is a 400-foot pipelay derrick barge capable of laying
48-inch diameter pipe using the conventional method. The <B><I>Iroquois </I></B>is currently assigned to our
Latin America segment.
</DIV>



<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt"><!-- Folio -->21<!-- /Folio -->
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always"><A HREF="#H79937tocpage">Table of Contents</A></H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">





<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">The <B><I>DLB 264</I></B>, a major construction vessel, is a 400-foot pipelay derrick barge capable of lifts up
to approximately 1,100 tons used in the conventional method of pipelay. The <B><I>DLB 264 </I></B>can lay pipe
up to 60 inches in diameter and is currently assigned to our Asia Pacific/Middle East segment.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">The <B><I>Chickasaw</I></B>, a major construction vessel, is a 275-foot dynamically positioned pipelay barge
with a pipelay reel that has a capacity ranging from forty-five miles of 4.5-inch diameter pipe,
nineteen miles of 6.625-inch diameter pipe or four miles of 12.75-inch diameter pipe in one
continuous length. The <B><I>Chickasaw </I></B>is currently stationed in the U.S. Gulf of Mexico.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">The <B><I>Pioneer</I></B>, an MSV, is a dynamically positioned small waterplane area twin hull (SWATH)&nbsp;vessel
that provides support services in water depths to 8,000 feet. The <B><I>Pioneer</I>&#146;s </B>hull design reduces
weather sensitivity, allowing the vessel to continue operating in up to 12-foot seas and remain on
site in up to 20-foot seas. The vessel is equipped to install, maintain, and service subsea
completions and support saturation diving. It is also equipped for abandonment operations,
pipeline installation support, and other services beyond the capabilities of conventional DSVs.
The <B><I>Pioneer </I></B>is currently stationed in the U. S. Gulf of Mexico.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">The <B><I>Normand Commander</I></B>, an MSV, is a 305-foot dynamically positioned dive support vessel. This
vessel has extensive capabilities, including dynamic positioning, 100-ton crane capacity with
deepwater lowering capability to 6,500 feet and specialized design features which facilitate
diving, ROV inspection, and other offshore construction services. The <B><I>Normand Commander</I></B>, which is
currently assigned to our North America OCD segment in the U.S. Gulf of Mexico, was delivered in
June&nbsp;2006 under a long-term charter with a five-year fixed term and five one-year options.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">The <B><I>Olympic Challenger</I></B>, an MSV, is a DP-2 class 347-foot dynamically positioned self-propelled MSV
and inspection, repair and maintenance (IRM)&nbsp;vessel. It is equipped with a 250-ton heave
compensated crane with deepwater lowering capability to 9,800 feet. We leased the <B><I>Olympic
Challenger </I></B>in August&nbsp;2008 from a third party under a charter agreement with a fixed term of five
years with one two-year option and three one-year options. Constructed in 2008 and equipped with a
Schilling ROV system, the vessel is designed for performance of field development and ROV/SURF
work. The <B><I>Olympic Challenger </I></B>is currently stationed in the U. S. Gulf of Mexico.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">The <B><I>Global Orion</I></B>, an MSV, is a DP-2 class 299-foot dynamically positioned self-propelled MSV. It
was constructed in 2002 and includes a 150-ton crane with deepwater lowering capability to 4,900
feet and a saturation diving system. The vessel is designed for diving support as well as
performance of field development and SURF work. The <B><I>Global Orion </I></B>is currently stationed in the U.
S. Gulf of Mexico.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">The <B><I>Global 1200</I></B>, a major construction vessel, recently entered our fleet upon the completion of its
construction and subsequent sea trials. The <B><I>Global 1200 </I></B>is a next generation multi-purpose DP-2
construction vessel designed for work in both deep and shallow water. The vessel incorporates a
state-of-the-art pipelay system capable of handling 60-inch concrete coated pipe. The <B><I>Global 1200</I></B>
also incorporates a 1,200 metric ton capacity crane suitable for conventional platform
installations.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">The <B><I>Global 1201 </I></B>is currently under construction and should enter our fleet in late 2011. The
<B><I>Global 1201 </I></B>is comparable to the <B><I>Global 1200</I></B>.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">We own nine of the vessels in the operating fleet described above. The <B><I>Titan 2</I></B>, <B><I>Olympic
Challenger</I></B>, and <B><I>Normand Commander </I></B>are leased vessels. Six of the vessels that we own are subject
to ship mortgages. In compliance with governmental regulations, our insurance policies, and
certain of our financing arrangements, we are required to maintain our vessels in accordance with
standards of seaworthiness and safety set by government regulations or classification
organizations. We maintain our fleet to the standards for seaworthiness, safety, and health set by
the International Maritime Organization or the U.S. Coast Guard, and our vessels are inspected by
the American Bureau of Shipping, Bureau Veritas, or Det Norske Veritas.
</DIV>




<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt"><!-- Folio -->22<!-- /Folio -->
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always"><A HREF="#H79937tocpage">Table of Contents</A></H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">




<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">We also own and operate other ancillary offshore construction equipment including portable pipe
reels, jetting sleds, and hydraulic and steam pile driving hammers. Our portable pipe reels are
relatively small vertical reels which can be used for small diameter pipe on conventional barges or
for simultaneously laying two pipelines using the <B><I>Chickasaw</I></B>. Our jetting sleds are capable of
burying pipelines of up to thirty-six inches in diameter. Pile hammers are used by construction
barges to drive the pilings which secure offshore platforms to the bottom of the sea. We also
operate nine saturation diving systems. Our saturation systems range in capacity from six to
twelve divers. Four of the saturation systems are capable of supporting dives as deep as 1,000
feet. Each saturation system consists of a diving bell, for transporting the divers to the sea
floor, and pressurized living quarters. The systems have surface controls for measuring and mixing
the specialized gases that the divers breathe and connecting hatches for entering the diving bell
and providing meals and supplies to the divers.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">In addition to the fleet of vessels which we operate as described above, we charter other vessels,
such as tugboats, cargo barges, utility boats, and dive support vessels, and lease other equipment,
such as saturation diving systems and ROVs.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">We own 603 acres near Carlyss, Louisiana and have constructed a deepwater support facility and pipe
base. This location serves as the headquarters of our North America Offshore Construction and
Subsea operations. The facility is capable of accommodating our deepwater draft vessels.
</DIV>

</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>ITEM 3. LEGAL PROCEEDINGS</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">We are involved in various legal proceedings and potential claims that arise in the ordinary course
of business, primarily involving claims for personal injury under the General Maritime Laws of the
United States and Jones Act as a result of alleged negligence. We believe that the outcome of all
such proceedings, even if determined adversely, would not have a material adverse effect on our
business or financial statements. For more information, please see the information set forth under
the heading &#147;Litigation&#148; in Note 10, &#147;Commitments and Contingencies,&#148; to our consolidated financial
statements included in this Annual Report.
</DIV>


<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt"><!-- Folio -->25<!-- /Folio -->
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Mr.&nbsp;Reed joined the Company in March&nbsp;2010 with more than thirty years experience in the offshore
construction industry. Prior to joining the Company, he served as Chief Executive Officer of
Heerema Marine Contractors after holding a number of other senior roles with the Heerema Group
including Chief Executive Officer of INTEC Engineering, Inc. He previously held a number of other
management roles at Heerema in project management, business development, and engineering
capacities. Mr.&nbsp;Reed holds a Bachelors degree in Engineering from the University of Mississippi
and an MBA from Delta State University. He previously served as a member of the Board of Directors
of the National Ocean Industries Association and is a past President of the International Pipeline
and Marine Contractors Association and a past Chairman of the International Marine Contractors
Association, America&#146;s Deepwater Division.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Mr.&nbsp;Smith joined the Company in April&nbsp;2010. Prior to joining the Company, he served in a number of
financial executive roles, most recently as Senior Vice President and Chief Financial Officer, with
NATCO Group. He previously was a financial executive with Comfort Systems USA and the public
accounting firm of Pricewaterhouse Coopers. Mr.&nbsp;Smith is a graduate of the University of Houston
and a Certified Public Accountant in the State of Texas.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Mr.&nbsp;Robicheaux joined the Company in August&nbsp;1999 as Vice President and General Counsel. In August
2001, he was named Senior Vice President, General Counsel. In April&nbsp;2006, Mr.&nbsp;Robicheaux was named
Chief Administrative Officer and General Counsel. Prior to joining the Company, Mr.&nbsp;Robicheaux had
been Assistant General Counsel with J. Ray McDermott, S.A. since 1995. In addition, he served in
various engineering and legal capacities at McDermott International, Inc. for the preceding
twenty-five years, including design and field engineering, project engineering, estimating and
project management. Mr.&nbsp;Robicheaux holds a Bachelors degree in Electrical Engineering from
Louisiana State University and a J.D. from Loyola University.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Mr.&nbsp;Ashit Jain joined the Company in 1997 and has held top management positions in operations,
engineering, project management, estimating and project controls. He was appointed Senior Vice
President, Asia Pacific &#038; India in 2006 and was based in the Asia Pacific region. In August&nbsp;2010,
Mr.
</DIV>


<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt"><!-- Folio -->26<!-- /Folio -->
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always"><A HREF="#H79937tocpage">Table of Contents</A></H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">




<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Jain was named Chief Operating Officer. Mr.&nbsp;Jain has over eighteen years experience in the marine
construction industry. Mr.&nbsp;Jain holds a Bachelor&#146;s degree in Civil Engineering from the University
of Delhi in India and an MBA from the University of Houston.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Mr.&nbsp;Osborn joined the Company in June&nbsp;2010 with responsibility for worldwide business acquisition
and development, proposals and estimating, marketing, and related commercial activities. He has
more than thirty years experience in business development, operations, and engineering roles, most
recently as Senior Vice President, Business Development with IntecSea, a unit of the worldwide
engineering firm of Worley Parsons. Earlier in his career, Mr.&nbsp;Osborn spent seventeen years with
Brown &#038; Root, Inc. and its successor, KBR, serving in various commercial, business development, and
operations positions. Mr.&nbsp;Osborn holds both a Bachelors degree and a Masters degree in Engineering
from the Massachusetts Institute of Technology.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Mr.&nbsp;James Dor&#233; has over twenty-seven years of service with the Company. He has held a number of
management positions with responsibility for marketing, contracts and estimating, and diving
operations. Mr.&nbsp;Dor&#233; was named Vice President, Marketing in March&nbsp;1993, Vice President, Special
Services in November&nbsp;1994 and Vice President, Diving and Special Services in February&nbsp;1996. In
August&nbsp;2001, he was named Senior Vice President, Diving and Special Services. In November&nbsp;2002,
Mr.&nbsp;Dor&#233; was named President of Global Divers and Marine Contractors. In June&nbsp;2005, Mr.&nbsp;Dor&#233; was
appointed Senior Vice President, Asia Pacific/India. In October&nbsp;2006, Mr.&nbsp;Dor&#233; was named Senior
Vice President, Eastern Hemisphere. In July&nbsp;2007, he was named Senior Vice President, Worldwide
Diving and Subsea Services and Middle East and Mediterranean Region. In November&nbsp;2008, Mr.&nbsp;Dor&#233;
relinquished his role over the Middle East and Mediterranean Region and assumed responsibility for
the North America Region. In May&nbsp;2010, he relinquished his role over the North America Region to
focus on worldwide opportunities and in September&nbsp;2010, he was named Senior Vice President,
Worldwide Diving. Mr.&nbsp;Dor&#233; previously served as President of the Association of Diving
Contractors, an industry trade association. Mr.&nbsp;Dor&#233; is the brother of William J. Dor&#233;, our
founder and a member of our Board of Directors.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Mr.&nbsp;Baker joined the Company in May&nbsp;1997 as Operations Manager in our Latin America segment and
served in various capacities with us through August&nbsp;2001. In August&nbsp;2001, Mr.&nbsp;Baker was named
Senior Vice President of Equipment, Operations and Regulatory and in 2003 was named Senior Vice
President, the Americas. In June&nbsp;2007, he was named Senior Vice President, North America Region
and Worldwide Fleet. In August&nbsp;2009, Mr.&nbsp;Baker was named Senior Vice President, Worldwide
Operations and in May&nbsp;2010, he was named Senior Vice President, Operations and Fleet Management.
Prior to joining the Company, Mr.&nbsp;Baker served in various management capacities for J. Ray
McDermott, Inc., Offshore Pipelines, Inc. and Brown &#038; Root, Inc. Mr.&nbsp;Baker has more than
thirty-two years of experience in the offshore construction industry including service in the
United States, Latin America, the North Sea, West Africa, Asia Pacific/India, and the Middle East.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Mr.&nbsp;Borja rejoined the Company in January&nbsp;2009 as Senior Vice President, Global Marketing and
Strategy with responsibility for market analysis and development of growth strategies to expand our
services into deepwater and subsea markets. He was appointed Senior Vice President, Strategic
Planning in October&nbsp;2010. Mr.&nbsp;Borja first joined us in September&nbsp;2001, serving in leadership
positions in both operations and business development for the Latin America segment. He was named
Vice President, Latin America in May&nbsp;2002 and served in that role until mid-2008. His operational
and business development experience includes domestic and international assignments, including
positions with ICA-Fluor Daniel. Mr.&nbsp;Borja holds a Master&#146;s Degree in Construction Management from
Universidad Iberoamericana and a Bachelors Degree in Civil Engineering from Universidad Nacional
Autonoma de Mexico.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Mr.&nbsp;Sheil joined the Company in January&nbsp;2008 as Vice President, Human Resources. In September
2009, he was promoted to Senior Vice President, Human Resources, where he has responsibility for
employee relations, compensation and benefits, organizational development and succession planning.
Prior to joining the Company, Mr.&nbsp;Sheil served in various management capacities for more than
twenty years with Cooper Industries, PLC, a multinational industrial manufacturing firm. His most
recent position with Cooper was Senior Vice President, Human Resources. Mr.&nbsp;Sheil holds a B.S. degree in
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Mrs.&nbsp;Trudy McConnaughhay joined the Company in February&nbsp;1999 as Assistant Corporate Controller and

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">As of February&nbsp;15, 2011, there were approximately 657 holders of record of our common stock and

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">We have never paid cash dividends on our common stock, and we do not intend to pay cash dividends
in the foreseeable future. We currently intend to retain earnings, if any, for the future
operation and growth of our business. Our Revolving Credit Facility and other financing
arrangements restrict the payment of cash dividends.

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">





<DIV align="left">
<A name="H79937111"></A>
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="margin-top: 12pt">
<TABLE width="100%" border="0" cellpadding="0" cellspacing="0" style="font-size: 10pt; background: transparent; color: #000000">
<TR>
    <TD width="3%"></TD>
    <TD width="1%"></TD>
    <TD></TD>
</TR>
<TR valign="top">
    <TD nowrap align="left"><B>ITEM 7.</B></TD>
    <TD>&nbsp;</TD>
    <TD><B>MANAGEMENT&#146;S DISCUSSION AND ANALYSIS OF FINANCIAL CONDITION AND RESULTS OF OPERATIONS</B></TD>
</TR>
</TABLE>
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">The following discussion presents management&#146;s discussion and analysis of our financial condition
and results of operations and should be read in conjunction with the Consolidated Financial
Statements and the related &#147;Notes to Consolidated Financial Statements&#148; included in Item&nbsp;8 of Part
II of this Annual Report.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Results of Operations</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><B>General</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Our reportable segments consist of North America OCD, North America Subsea, Latin America,
West Africa, and Asia Pacific/Middle East.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Effective January&nbsp;1, 2010, we combined our Middle East and Asia Pacific/India segments into the
Asia Pacific/Middle East segment. The equipment and personnel assigned to each of these segments,
as well as the executive management thereof, were consolidated during 2009; therefore, we made the
decision to combine the reporting segments. The combined reporting segment continues to pursue
projects in both regions. During the first quarter of 2009, we discontinued allocation of
corporate stewardship costs to our reportable segments. These changes are reflected as
retrospective changes to the financial information and the narrative description in &#147;Management&#146;s
Discussion and Analysis of Results of Operations and Financial Condition&#148; presented for the years
ended December&nbsp;31, 2010, 2009 and 2008. These changes did not have an impact on our consolidated
balance sheets, consolidated statements of operations, consolidated statements of equity, or
consolidated statements of cash flows.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Our results of operations are affected by the overall level of activity of the offshore
construction industry within each geographic region in which we operate. The overall level of
offshore construction activity is principally determined by four factors: (1)&nbsp;the oil and gas
industry&#146;s ability to economically justify placing discoveries of oil and gas reserves in
production; (2)&nbsp;the oil and gas industry&#146;s need to maintain, repair, and replace existing pipelines
and structures to extend the life of production; (3)&nbsp;the oil and gas industry&#146;s need to clear
structures from the lease once the oil and gas reserves have been depleted; and (4)&nbsp;weather events
such as major hurricanes. Our results of operations ultimately reflect our ability to secure jobs
through competitive bidding and manage those jobs to successful completion. The competition and
inherent operating risks vary between the geographic regions in which we operate, and these
challenges affect individual segment profitability.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Our results of operations are measured in terms of revenues, gross profit, and gross profit as a
percentage of revenues (&#147;margins&#148;) and are principally driven by three factors: (1)&nbsp;our level of
offshore construction and subsea activity (&#147;activity&#148;), (2)&nbsp;pricing, which can be affected by
contract mix (&#147;pricing&#148;), and (3)&nbsp;operating efficiency on any particular construction project
(&#147;productivity&#148;).
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Offshore Construction Services</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">The level of our offshore construction activity in any given period has a significant impact
on our results of operations. The offshore construction business is capital and personnel
intensive, and, as a practical matter, many of our costs, including the wages of skilled workers,
are effectively fixed in the short run regardless of whether or not our vessels are being utilized
in productive service. In general, as activity increases, a greater proportion of these fixed
costs are recovered through operating revenues and, consequently, gross profit and margins
increase. Conversely, as activity decreases, our revenues decline, but our costs do not decline
proportionally, thereby constricting our gross profit and margins. Our activity level can be
affected by changes in demand due to economic or other conditions in the oil and gas exploration
industry, seasonal conditions in certain geographical areas, and/or our ability to win the bidding
for available jobs. Our results of operations depend heavily upon our ability to obtain, in a very
competitive environment, a sufficient quantity of offshore construction contracts with sufficient
gross profit margins to recover the fixed costs associated with our offshore construction business.
</DIV>


<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt"><!-- Folio -->31<!-- /Folio -->
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always"><A HREF="#H79937tocpage">Table of Contents</A></H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">




<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Most of our offshore construction revenues are obtained through international contracts which are
generally larger, more complex, and of longer duration than our typical domestic contracts. Most
of these international contracts require a significant amount of working capital, are generally bid
on a lump-sum basis, and are secured by a letter of credit or performance bond. Operating cash
flows may be negatively impacted during periods of escalating activity due to the substantial
amounts of cash required to initiate these projects and the normal delays between cash expenditures
by us and cash receipts from the customer. Additionally, lump-sum contracts for offshore
construction services are inherently risky and are subject to many unforeseen circumstances and
events that may affect productivity. When productivity decreases with no offsetting decrease in
costs or increases in revenues, our contract margins erode compared to our bid margins. In
general, we are required to bear a larger share of project related risks during periods of weak
demand for our services and a smaller share of risk during periods of high demand for our services.
Consequently, our revenues and margins from offshore construction services are subject to a high
degree of variability, even as compared to other businesses in the offshore energy industry.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Claims and change orders are a significant aspect of any construction business and are particularly
significant in the offshore construction industry. A claim is an amount in excess of the contract
price which a construction contractor seeks to collect from customers or others due to delays,
errors in specifications or design, unapproved change orders, or other causes of unanticipated
costs caused by the customer or others. A change order is a request to alter the scope of work of
a previously agreed upon construction contract. Change orders may include changes in
specifications or design, method or manner of performance, facilities, equipment, site, or the
period for completion of the work. Change orders are common in our business due to the nature of
offshore construction contracts and sometimes add to the degree of project execution difficulty. A
change order usually increases the scope of work but may also decrease the scope and, consequently,
the amount of contract revenue and costs which are recognized. Change orders can be initiated by
us or the customer. At the time of initiation, a change order may be approved or unapproved by
either party, priced or unpriced, or defined or undefined regarding detailed scope. Even when the
scope of work is defined, the associated increase or decrease in contract revenue may be governed
by contract terms or may be negotiated later, sometimes after the work is performed.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Subsea Services</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Most of our subsea revenues are the result of short-term work, involve numerous smaller
contracts, and are usually based on a day-rate charge. Financial risks associated with these types
of contracts are normally limited due to their short-term and non-lump sum nature. However, some
subsea contracts may involve longer-term commitments that extend from the exploration, design and
installation phases of a field throughout its useful life by providing IRM services. The financial
risks associated with these commitments are low in comparison with our offshore construction
activities due to the day-rate structure of the contracts. Revenues and margins from our subsea
activities tend to be more consistent than those from our offshore construction activities.
</DIV>


<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt"><!-- Folio -->32<!-- /Folio -->
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always"><A HREF="#H79937tocpage">Table of Contents</A></H5><P>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><B><I>Revenues </I></B>&#151; Revenues decreased by $346.2&nbsp;million, or 37.9%, from $914.3&nbsp;million for 2009 to $568.1
million for 2010 primarily due to lower activity and lower pricing in all reporting segments. For
a detailed discussion of revenues and income (loss)&nbsp;before taxes for each geographical area, please
see &#147;Segment Information&#148; below.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><B><I>Depreciation and Amortization in Cost of Operations </I></B>&#151; The amount of depreciation and amortization
expense, including the amortization of dry-docking costs, included in our cost of operations for
2010 was $43.6&nbsp;million, compared to $55.5&nbsp;million included for 2009. This $11.9&nbsp;million decrease
in expense was primarily attributable to decreased dry-dock amortization and lower
units-of-production (UOP)&nbsp;depreciation due to vessel sales and decreased vessel utilization in
2010. Partially offsetting these decreases was the increase in amortization of leasehold
improvements to one of our leased vessels. Stock-based compensation expense decreased between the
periods by $0.7&nbsp;million. We expect amortization of our dry-dock costs to be material to our
operations in 2011.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><B><I>Gross Profit </I></B>&#151; Gross profit decreased by $126.2&nbsp;million between 2010 and 2009 to $32.8&nbsp;million for
2010 compared to $159.0&nbsp;million for 2009. This decrease was primarily due to lower revenues and
higher non-recovered vessel costs attributable to decreased project activity, lower overall pricing
for our services, and start up costs associated with preparing the <B><I>Global 1200 </I></B>for use. Profits
from our Latin America segment were lower for 2010 due to lower pricing and low productivity on two
projects in Mexico resulting in combined estimated project losses of $17.8&nbsp;million. Lower profits
in our West Africa segment were primarily attributable to idle vessel costs coupled with no project
activity since our curtailment of operations in the region in mid-2009. Our Asia Pacific/Middle
East segment experienced lower profits due to decreased revenues and higher non-recovered vessel
costs in the region, as a result of lower project activity. Our North America Subsea segment was
negatively affected by lower pricing, dry-docking of the <B><I>Pioneer </I></B>and lower project activity for the
<B><I>Global Orion, Sea Cat </I></B>and <B><I>Sea Fox</I></B>. The <B><I>Global Orion </I></B>was undergoing major repairs to its crane in
early 2010 and was unavailable for work until late May. The <B><I>Sea Cat </I></B>and <B><I>Sea Fox </I></B>were removed from
our operating fleet in the first quarter of 2010 and subsequently sold. Partially offsetting these
declines was increased project activity associated with the <B><I>Olympic Challenger </I></B>and <B><I>Normand Commander. </I></B>Our North America
OCD segment was
</DIV>



<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt"><!-- Folio -->33<!-- /Folio -->
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always"><A HREF="#H79937tocpage">Table of Contents</A></H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">




<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">negatively affected by lower pricing and lower project activity for the <B><I>Hercules</I></B>
and <B><I>Cherokee </I></B>and the <B><I>Sea Constructor</I></B>, which was sold in July&nbsp;2010.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><B><I>Goodwill </I></B>&#151; In the third quarter of 2010, we tested the goodwill of the Latin America and North
America OCD reporting units for potential impairment in light of losses incurred during the period
and weaker outlook for future performance. Consequently, we recognized a goodwill impairment of
$37.4&nbsp;million. Of the total impairment, $1.1&nbsp;million was related to our North America OCD segment
and $36.3&nbsp;million was related to our Latin America segment.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><B><I>Loss on Other Asset Impairments &#151; </I></B>Loss on other asset impairments increased $62.9&nbsp;million in 2010,
compared to 2009. In 2010, we recorded a $43.8&nbsp;million impairment of the <B><I>Hercules </I></B>as a result of
our annual vessel impairment review. Due to the decline in market rates for this vessel, its book
value at December&nbsp;31, 2010 exceeded its fair market value resulting in the impairment. In February
2011, we gave notice to the lessor of the <B><I>Titan 2 </I></B>of our intent to terminate the lease effective in
the second quarter of 2011. Consequently, the carrying amount of the
related leasehold improvements and deferred dry-dock costs were
written down to their fair value and we recorded a $7.9&nbsp;million impairment.
In addition, in 2010, we re-measured the
fair value of three DSVs, a dive system and other equipment and recorded an aggregate impairment
loss on these assets of $12.4&nbsp;million. In comparison, in 2009, we retired two DSVs and three dive
systems and recorded an aggregate impairment loss of $1.2&nbsp;million.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><B><I>Gain on Asset Disposal </I></B>&#151; Net gains on the disposal of assets increased $22.9&nbsp;million in 2010,
compared to 2009, primarily due to the gains recognized on the sale of seven vessels as part of the
rationalization of our fleet in 2010 to better support larger, more complex projects. Net gains on
asset disposals totaled $31.3&nbsp;million in 2010 primarily from the sale of four DLBs, one DSV, one
OSV, and one cargo barge. In comparison, net gains on asset disposals totaled $8.4&nbsp;million in 2009
primarily from the sale of one DSV, one cargo barge, one DLB, and one pipelay/bury barge.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><B><I>Selling, General and Administrative Expenses </I></B>&#151; Selling, general and administrative expenses
increased by $1.1&nbsp;million to $70.3&nbsp;million for 2010, compared to $69.2&nbsp;million for 2009. Increased
labor costs of $3.8&nbsp;million in our Latin America and Corporate segments was the primary driver of
this increase along with increased equity compensation of $2.7&nbsp;million. Additionally, in 2010, we
initiated a relocation and severance plan with a number of our employees located in our Carlyss,
Louisiana office. Many of these employees are being relocated to our Houston, Texas office as part
of our plan to create a centralized organization structure. Selling, general and administrative
expenses for 2010 include $1.0&nbsp;million in relocation and severance expenses related to this plan.
Partially offsetting these increases were the decreased expenses of $5.5&nbsp;million for legal,
accounting, and other professional fees in 2010.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><B><I>Interest Income </I></B>&#151; Interest income decreased by $0.5&nbsp;million to $1.5&nbsp;million for 2010, compared to
$2.0&nbsp;million for 2009. Lower interest rates and cash balances in 2010 contributed to lower returns
on cash balances and short-term investments compared to 2009.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><B><I>Interest Expense </I></B>&#151; Interest expense decreased to $9.7&nbsp;million for 2010 compared to $13.1&nbsp;million
for 2009. This decrease of $3.4&nbsp;million was primarily due to higher capitalized interest related
to expenditures for construction of the <B><I>Global 1200 </I></B>and <B><I>Global 1201</I></B>. Capitalized interest for 2010
was $17.9&nbsp;million compared to $14.7&nbsp;million for 2009.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><B><I>Other Income (Expense), Net </I></B>&#151; Other expense, net was $0.6&nbsp;million for 2010, compared to other
income, net of $7.3&nbsp;million for 2009. We incurred losses of $0.5&nbsp;million on the sale of auction
rate securities and $0.5&nbsp;million on foreign currency exchange rate transactions in 2010, compared
to gains of $3.6&nbsp;million on foreign currency exchange rate transactions in 2009. In addition, we
received proceeds of $2.7&nbsp;million in 2009 from insurance claims.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><B><I>Income Taxes </I></B>&#151; Our effective tax rate was 18.4% and 21.0%, respectively, for the years ended
December&nbsp;31, 2010 and 2009. Our tax rate is affected by recurring items, such as tax rates in
foreign jurisdictions and the relative amount of income we earn, or losses we incur, in those
jurisdictions. It is also affected by discrete items that may occur in any given year, but are not
consistent from year to
</DIV>


<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt"><!-- Folio -->34<!-- /Folio -->
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always"><A HREF="#H79937tocpage">Table of Contents</A></H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">




<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">year. The tax rate in 2010 was affected by losses we incurred in high tax
jurisdictions that were tax benefited partially offset by income we earned in low tax jurisdictions
and the goodwill impairment and losses in our Latin America segment that were not  tax benefitted.
Comparatively, 2009 was profitable and benefited from higher earnings in foreign jurisdictions with
deemed profit tax regimes and utilization of losses not previously tax benefitted.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><B><I>Segment Information </I></B>&#151; The following sections discuss the results of operations for each of our
reportable segments during the years ended December&nbsp;31, 2010 and 2009.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B><I>North America Offshore Construction Division</I></B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Revenues were $60.3&nbsp;million for 2010 compared to $124.7&nbsp;million for 2009. The decrease of $64.4
million, or 52%, between 2010 and 2009 was primarily due to lower pricing due to competitive
bidding and lower project activity for the <B><I>Cherokee </I></B>and <B><I>Hercules, </I></B>and for the <B><I>Sea Constructor,</I></B>
which was sold in July&nbsp;2010, and lower project activity for the <B><I>Chickasaw. </I></B>The decline in project
activity was partially attributable to the permitting delays experienced as a consequence of the
April&nbsp;2010 Macondo oil spill in the U.S. Gulf of Mexico. Loss before taxes was $52.4&nbsp;million for
2010 compared to $1.3&nbsp;million for 2009. This $51.1&nbsp;million difference in loss before taxes is
primarily attributable to asset impairments in 2010. In 2010, we recorded a $1.1&nbsp;million
impairment of goodwill, a $5.0&nbsp;million impairment on the <B><I>Hercules </I></B>reel upon its classification to
Assets held for sale, and a $43.8&nbsp;million impairment on the <B><I>Hercules. </I></B>The book value of the
<B><I>Hercules </I></B>was tested for recoverability based upon its estimated discounted future cash flows. Due
to the decline in market rates for this vessel, its book value exceeded its estimated fair value by
$43.8&nbsp;million, resulting in an impairment charge related to this vessel. These losses were
partially offset by the $3.6&nbsp;million gain recognized on the sale of the <B><I>Sea Constructor </I></B>and the <B><I>CB6</I></B>
in 2010.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B><I>North America Subsea</I></B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Revenues were $135.0&nbsp;million for 2010 compared to $158.9&nbsp;million for 2009, a decrease of $23.9
million, or 15%. The decrease was primarily attributable to lower project activity for the <B><I>Global
Orion </I></B>and <B><I>Pioneer, </I></B>partially attributable to the permitting delays experienced as a consequence of
the April&nbsp;2010 Macondo oil spill in the U.S. Gulf of Mexico. Also, the <B><I>Pioneer </I></B>was in dry-dock for
the first quarter of 2010 and the <B><I>Global Orion </I></B>was undergoing major repairs to its crane and was
unavailable for work until late May&nbsp;2010. Partially offsetting these declines was higher project
activity for the <B><I>Olympic Challenger, Normand Commander</I></B>, and <B><I>Sea Leopard</I></B>. The <B><I>Olympic Challenger</I></B>
and <B><I>Normand Commander </I></B>were utilized as response vessels to the oil spill in the U.S. Gulf of
Mexico. The <B><I>Normand Commander </I></B>was assigned to our Latin America segment and returned to the U.S.
Gulf of Mexico in May&nbsp;2009 but experienced little activity in 2009. Income before taxes was $4.0
million for 2010 compared to $34.9&nbsp;million for 2009. The decrease of $30.9&nbsp;million in income
before taxes was primarily attributable to lower overall project margins attributable to lower
activity in the region. In addition, the results for 2009 included a $4.9&nbsp;million gain on the sale
of the <B><I>Sea Lion</I></B>.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B><I>Latin America</I></B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Revenues increased to $232.6&nbsp;million for 2010 compared to $229.3&nbsp;million for 2009. Loss before
taxes was $62.5&nbsp;million for 2010 compared to income before taxes of $8.2&nbsp;million for 2009. This
difference of $70.7&nbsp;million was primarily attributable to project losses in Mexico, goodwill
impairment of $36.3&nbsp;million, and a $7.9&nbsp;million impairment of the leasehold improvements and
deferred dry-dock costs associated with the cancellation of the <B><I>Titan 2 </I></B>lease in February&nbsp;2011. We
experienced project losses of $17.8&nbsp;million for the Line 58 and Line 59 projects for Pemex in
Mexico in 2010 primarily due to lower than expected productivity and vessel standby delays from
non-compensable weather downtime. Due to these project deteriorations, the results for 2010 include an estimate for losses on both
the Line 58 and Line 59 projects through their estimated completion date in the first quarter of
2011. Partially offsetting these decreases was a $9.5&nbsp;million gain on the sale of the <B><I>Shawnee</I></B>.
</DIV>


<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt"><!-- Folio -->35<!-- /Folio -->
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always"><A HREF="#H79937tocpage">Table of Contents</A></H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">




<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B><I>West Africa</I></B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Revenues were $(0.9) million in 2010 compared to revenues of $104.3&nbsp;million for 2009. Income
before taxes was $6.2&nbsp;million for 2010 compared to $27.5&nbsp;million for 2009. Activity in 2009
consisted of the completion of a large construction project for the replacement and repair of a
24-inch pipeline offshore Nigeria. Subsequent to the completion of that project in the second
quarter of 2009, we curtailed our operations in the region and have had no project activity in West
Africa since that time. In late 2010, we reserved $0.9&nbsp;million in income related to outstanding
accounts receivable on a prior year project. The income before taxes for 2010 was primarily due to
the $11.6&nbsp;million gain recognized on the sale of the <B><I>Cheyenne </I></B>and <B><I>Tornado, </I></B>partially offset by
non-recovered vessel costs associated with these vessels, which remained idle in Tema, Ghana until
their sale in September&nbsp;2010. The income before taxes for 2009 was attributable to (1)&nbsp;project
profitability related to the construction project in Nigeria, (2)&nbsp;gains on the sale of the <B><I>Sea
Puma</I></B>, <B><I>CB3</I></B>, and the <B><I>Power Barge 1</I></B>, (3)&nbsp;a $3.3&nbsp;million settlement with a customer for recovery of the
deterioration of the Nigerian naira on invoice payments, and (4)&nbsp;the receipt of an insurance
reimbursement of $1.8&nbsp;million related to prior year costs incurred on a project claim.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B><I>Asia Pacific/Middle East</I></B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Revenues were $165.0&nbsp;million for 2010 compared to $329.5&nbsp;million for 2009. This decrease of $164.5
million, or 50%, for 2010 compared to 2009 was the result of decreased project activity in the
region. Activity in 2010 consisted of two construction projects in Malaysia and one project in
Indonesia compared to four major construction projects in India, Indonesia, Saudi Arabia, and
Thailand in 2009. Income before taxes was $25.1&nbsp;million for 2010 compared to $50.2&nbsp;million for
2009. This decrease in income before taxes of $25.1&nbsp;million was primarily attributable to
decreased revenues and higher non-recovered vessel costs due to a decrease in project activity. In
addition, in 2010, we recorded impairments of $5.8&nbsp;million on the revaluation of the <B><I>Subtec 1 </I></B>and
other equipment held for sale. These items were partially offset by the $6.4&nbsp;million gain
recognized on the sale of the <B><I>DLB 332 </I></B>in 2010. The results for 2009 benefitted from $20.4&nbsp;million
of productivity improvements and costs savings on the Berri and Qatif project in Saudi Arabia and
gains of $3.8&nbsp;million on the sale of the <B><I>Seminole </I></B>and <B><I>Tonkawa</I></B>.
</DIV>


<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt"><!-- Folio -->36<!-- /Folio -->
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><B><I>Revenues </I></B>&#151; Revenues decreased by $156.7&nbsp;million, or 14.6%, between 2009 and 2008 to $914.3&nbsp;million
for 2009 primarily due to lower activity in the Asia Pacific/Middle East, West Africa, and Latin
America regions. This decrease was partially offset by a higher demand for our services in North
America. For a detailed discussion of revenues and income (loss)&nbsp;before taxes for each
geographical area, please see &#147;Segment Information&#148; below.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><B><I>Depreciation and Amortization in Cost of Operations </I></B>&#151; The amount of depreciation and amortization
expense, including the amortization of dry-docking costs, included in our cost of operations for
2009 was $55.5&nbsp;million, compared to $51.9&nbsp;million included in 2008. This increase in depreciation
and amortization expense was primarily caused by increased dry-dock amortization related to two
major construction vessels. Amortization of leasehold improvements to two of our leased vessels
also contributed to the increase in depreciation and amortization. Stock-based compensation expense
decreased between the periods by $1.4&nbsp;million.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><B><I>Gross Profit </I></B>&#151; Gross profit increased by $172.6&nbsp;million between 2009 and 2008 to a $159.0&nbsp;million
gross profit for 2009 compared to a $13.6&nbsp;million gross loss for 2008. Gross profit for 2008 was
adversely affected by the losses incurred on the Berri and Qatif project in Saudi Arabia and the
Camarupim project in Brazil. Cost overruns on the Berri and Qatif project and productivity and
equipment delays on both projects resulted in substantial project deterioration during 2008.
Higher activity in North America OCD and North America Subsea and higher margins in all segments,
except Asia Pacific/Middle East, contributed to the increase.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><B><I>Loss on Asset Impairments &#151; </I></B>Loss on asset impairments decreased $1.4&nbsp;million in 2009 compared to
2008. During 2009, primarily due to repair costs exceeding the future expected benefit of certain
vessels, we retired two DSVs and three dive systems and recorded an aggregate impairment loss of
$1.2&nbsp;million compared to an impairment loss in 2008 of $2.6&nbsp;million on the retirement of two DSVs.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><B><I>Gain on Asset Disposal </I></B>&#151; Net gains on the disposal of assets increased $6.7&nbsp;million from 2008
to 2009. Net gains on asset disposals totaled $8.4&nbsp;million for 2009 primarily from the sale of one
DSV, one cargo barge, one DLB, and one pipelay/bury barge. Net gains on asset disposals totaled
$1.7&nbsp;million for 2008 primarily from the sale of a DSV.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><B><I>Selling, General and Administrative Expenses </I></B>&#151; Selling, general and administrative expenses
decreased by $26.2&nbsp;million, or 28%, to $69.2&nbsp;million for 2009, compared to $95.4&nbsp;million during
2008.
</DIV>


<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt"><!-- Folio -->37<!-- /Folio -->
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always"><A HREF="#H79937tocpage">Table of Contents</A></H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">




<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Decreased labor costs of $7.7&nbsp;million in all areas except North America OCD was the primary
driver of this decrease as well as decreases in travel costs, stock-based compensation expense, and
legal and professional fees. These decreases were the result of our ongoing cost reduction
efforts.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><B><I>Interest Income </I></B>&#151; Interest income decreased by $12.5&nbsp;million to $2.0&nbsp;million during 2009, compared
to $14.5&nbsp;million for 2008. Significantly lower interest rates in 2009 contributed to lower returns
on cash balances and short-term investments compared to 2008.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><B><I>Interest Expense </I></B>&#151; Interest expense decreased to $13.1&nbsp;million for 2009 compared to $16.4&nbsp;million
for 2008. Higher capitalized interest primarily driven by expenditures for ongoing construction of
the <B><I>Global 1200 </I></B>and <B><I>Global 1201 </I></B>was responsible for the majority of the decrease. Capitalized
interest for 2009 was $14.7&nbsp;million compared to $7.3&nbsp;million for 2008. Interest expense for 2008
was affected by the reversal of $2.5&nbsp;million previously accrued interest expense related to the
settlement of a previously uncertain tax position.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><B><I>Other Income (Expense), Net </I></B>&#151; Other income (expense), net increased by $7.9&nbsp;million from 2008
primarily resulting from gains of $3.6&nbsp;million on foreign currency exchange rate transactions
incurred in 2009 compared to exchange losses of $1.9&nbsp;million in 2008 and from proceeds of $2.7
million in 2009 from insurance claims.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><B><I>Income Taxes </I></B>&#151; Our effective tax rate was 21.0% and (6.0)%, respectively, for the years ended
December&nbsp;31, 2009 and 2008. The tax rate in 2008 was adversely impacted by losses that could not
be tax benefited and by taxes paid in tax jurisdictions with a deemed profit tax regime where tax
is calculated as a percentage of revenue rather than being based upon net income, resulting in an
income tax expense being recognized despite the reported loss. Comparatively, 2009 was profitable
and benefitted from higher earnings in foreign jurisdictions with deemed profit tax regimes and
utilization of losses not previously tax benefitted.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><B><I>Segment Information </I></B>&#151; The following sections discuss the results of operations for each of our
reportable segments during the years ended December&nbsp;31, 2009 and 2008.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B><I>North America Offshore Construction Division</I></B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Revenues were $124.7&nbsp;million in 2009 compared to $81.1&nbsp;million in 2008. The increase of $43.6
million, or 54%, between 2009 and 2008 was primarily due to the relocation of the <B><I>Hercules </I></B>and <B><I>Sea
Constructor </I></B>to the U.S. Gulf of Mexico in early 2009 and increased utilization of the <B><I>Cherokee</I></B>.
The increased revenue was partially offset by the reduction in the utilization of (a)&nbsp;the <B><I>Titan II</I></B>,
which was on charter from our Latin America segment during 2008, (b)&nbsp;the <B><I>Chickasaw</I></B>, and (c)&nbsp;the
<B><I>GP37</I></B>. Revenues in 2008 were negatively affected by adverse weather conditions including two major
hurricanes and the extended dry-docking of the <B><I>Cherokee</I></B>. Loss before taxes was $1.3&nbsp;million for
2009 compared to $15.0&nbsp;million for 2008. This decrease in loss of $13.7&nbsp;million was primarily
attributable to higher vessel utilization and higher margins from increased productivity.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B><I>North America Subsea</I></B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Revenues generated in 2009 were $158.9&nbsp;million compared to $146.1&nbsp;million for 2008, an increase of
$12.8&nbsp;million, or 9%. The increase was primarily attributable to increased activity for two MSVs,
the <B><I>Olympic Challenger </I></B>and <B><I>Global Orion</I></B>, which entered service in the second half of 2008,
partially offset by the loss of revenue from the <B><I>Sea Lion </I></B>and a third party vessel used in 2008.
The <B><I>Sea Lion </I></B>was grounded in an incident in November&nbsp;2008, was damaged beyond economical repair,
and sold in the first quarter of 2009. Income before taxes was $34.9&nbsp;million for 2009 compared to
$11.3&nbsp;million for 2008. The increase of $23.6&nbsp;million was primarily attributable to higher
revenues and project margins due to improved pricing plus a $4.9&nbsp;million gain on the sale of the
<B><I>Sea Lion</I></B>. Partially offsetting the increase were idle costs attributable to the <B><I>Normand Commander </I></B>that was relocated to
the U.S. Gulf of Mexico in May&nbsp;2009. The project margins in 2008 were affected by competitive
pricing and productivity issues on some of our projects as well as idle and startup costs
associated with the addition
</DIV>



<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt"><!-- Folio -->38<!-- /Folio -->
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always"><A HREF="#H79937tocpage">Table of Contents</A></H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">




<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">of the <B><I>Global Orion </I></B>and <B><I>Olympic Challenger</I></B>. In addition, we recorded
a $1.0&nbsp;million impairment on a DSV during 2008.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B><I>Latin America</I></B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Revenues were $229.3&nbsp;million for 2009 compared to $267.0&nbsp;million for 2008. A decrease of $37.7
million, or 14%, in 2009 compared to 2008 was primarily due to lower activity in Brazil and Mexico.
Income before taxes was $8.2&nbsp;million during 2009 compared to a loss before taxes of $9.2&nbsp;million
in 2008. The increase of $17.4&nbsp;million was primarily attributable to higher project margins in
Brazil, partially offset by lower productivity on Mexico projects and lower vessel utilization in
Brazil and Mexico. We completed the Camarupim project in Brazil in 2009 after experiencing (a)
additional project deterioration of $4.0&nbsp;million attributable to increased costs associated with
rescheduling diving work from the <B><I>Normand Commander </I></B>to a third party diving vessel and (b)
increased project duration caused by third party equipment failure. In comparison, we recorded a
$30.1&nbsp;million loss on this project in 2008.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B><I>West Africa</I></B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Revenues were $104.3&nbsp;million for 2009 compared to $152.9&nbsp;million for 2008. This decrease of $48.6
million, or 32%, in 2009 compared to 2008 was primarily due to decreased activity attributable to
low demand for services in the region. We completed work on one construction project in 2009
compared to two major construction projects in 2008. Income before taxes was $27.5&nbsp;million for
2009 compared to a loss before taxes of $33.5&nbsp;million for 2008. This increase in income before
taxes of $61.0&nbsp;million was attributable to (a)&nbsp;increased project profitability due to increased
pricing and productivity, (b)&nbsp;reduced vessel costs with the transfer of the <B><I>Hercules </I></B>and <B><I>Sea
Constructor </I></B>to North America in January&nbsp;2009, (c)&nbsp;reduced vessel costs due to the sale of the <B><I>Sea
Puma</I></B>, <B><I>CB3</I></B>, and <B><I>Power Barge 1</I></B>, and (d)&nbsp;the reduction in labor, travel, and professional fees
attributable to our decision to curtail operations in the region. In addition, in 2009 we received
a $1.8&nbsp;million insurance reimbursement related to prior year costs incurred on a project claim and
reached a $3.3&nbsp;million settlement with a customer for recovery of the deterioration of the Nigerian
naira on remitted invoice payments and final payment of outstanding naira invoices in U.S. Dollars.
Negatively impacting the 2008 loss before taxes were (a)&nbsp;the additional costs incurred related to
idle vessel costs from low utilization and project productivity issues related to a project in
Nigeria, (b)&nbsp;a $1.6&nbsp;million impairment on a DSV, and (c)&nbsp;exchange losses of $3.7&nbsp;million primarily
related to naira cash balances. We began curtailment of our operations in this region beginning
in the second quarter of 2009 and have had no project activity in West Africa since that time.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B><I>Asia Pacific/Middle East</I></B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Revenues were $329.5&nbsp;million for 2009 compared to $457.3&nbsp;million during 2008. This decrease of
$127.8&nbsp;million, or 28%, for 2009 compared to 2008 was the result of lower project activity in the
region. Income before taxes was $50.2&nbsp;million during 2009 compared to a loss before taxes of $29.0
million for 2008. This increase in income before taxes of $79.2&nbsp;million was primarily attributable
to $20.4&nbsp;million of productivity improvements and cost savings on the Berri and Qatif project
compared to $82.3&nbsp;million loss on this project in 2008. Partially offsetting the 2008 loss on the
Berri and Qatif project were profits on two projects in India and charters of the <B><I>Seminole </I></B>and <B><I>DLB
264</I></B>. Reduced costs for labor and travel of $1.5&nbsp;million and gains of $3.8&nbsp;million on the sale of
the <B><I>Seminole </I></B>and <B><I>Tonkawa </I></B>also positively affected the income before taxes for 2009. Partially
offsetting these increases in 2009 was $2.4&nbsp;million in foreign currency exchange losses.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Utilization of Major Construction Vessels</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Worldwide utilization for our major construction vessels was 35%, 44%, and 49% for the fiscal
years ended December&nbsp;31, 2010, 2009, and 2008, respectively. Utilization of our major construction
vessels is
</DIV>


<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt"><!-- Folio -->39<!-- /Folio -->
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always"><A HREF="#H79937tocpage">Table of Contents</A></H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">




<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">calculated by dividing the total number of days major construction vessels are assigned
to project-related work by the total number of calendar days for the period. Dive support vessels,
cargo/launch barges, ancillary supply vessels and short-term chartered project-specific
construction vessels are excluded from the utilization calculation. We frequently use chartered
anchor handling tugs, dive support vessels and, from time to time, construction vessels in our
operations. Also, most of our international contracts (which are generally larger, more complex and
of longer duration) are generally bid on a lump-sum or unit-rate (vs. day-rate) basis wherein we
assume the risk of performance and changes in utilization rarely impact revenues but can have an
inverse relationship to changes in profitability. For these reasons, we consider utilization rates
to have a relatively low direct correlation to changes in revenue and gross profit.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Industry and Business Outlook</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Since the economic downturn that began in 2008, demand in our industry remains low. Supply in
the offshore construction industry continues to exceed demand on a worldwide basis. The ratio of
bids to available vessels is creating pricing pressures among competitors and is affecting our
ability to win new project awards. In addition, activity in the U.S. Gulf of Mexico has not
returned to normal levels following the Macondo well incident in April&nbsp;2010 and we cannot predict
the future impact this incident will have on our operations. Bid activity is increasing for
projects in 2012 and beyond, but we continue to expect weak demand for our services throughout
2011.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">During 2011, our focus will include successful execution of our projects, successful integration of
the <B><I>Global 1200 </I></B>and <B><I>1201 </I></B>into our fleet, building additional backlog, retaining and/or hiring key
personnel, and cash conservation. We continue to pursue new work; however, we have not yet been
successful in obtaining new project awards sufficient for the size of our existing operations. To
the extent that we are not successful in building sufficient backlog, further cost cutting and cash
conservation measures will be required, including closing offices, stacking idle vessels, asset
sales and reducing our work force further.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">As of December&nbsp;31, 2010, our backlog totaled approximately $170.8&nbsp;million ($166.9&nbsp;million for
international regions and $3.9&nbsp;million for North America). The entire backlog is scheduled to be
performed in 2011. The amount of our backlog in North America is not a reliable indicator of the
level of demand for our services due to the prevalence of short-term contractual arrangements in
this region.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Liquidity and Capital Resources</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><B>Overview</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Our cash and cash equivalents increased by $4.7&nbsp;million to $349.6&nbsp;million at December&nbsp;31, 2010,
compared to $344.9&nbsp;million at December&nbsp;31, 2009. Cash generated from operations, proceeds from the
sale of assets, the sale of marketable securities and advance deposits received on the sale of
assets provided the major sources of funds in 2010. These sources of funds sufficiently funded our
operations and capital expenditures for expanding and modernizing our fleet of vessels.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">The primary use of cash during 2010 has been the funding of capital spending. We had firm capital
commitments on projects, which were in progress at December&nbsp;31, 2010, of approximately $75.5
million, which is expected to be expended during 2011, primarily for the construction of the
remaining new derrick/pipelay vessel (the <B><I>Global 1201</I></B>) and two new saturation diving systems.
Total 2011 capital expenditures on committed and discretionary projects are expected to be
approximately $170.0 &#151; $180.0&nbsp;million. The actual capital expenditures for 2011 may differ from
our expectations due to changes in existing capital project schedules and/or projected capital
projects. We expect that balances of cash and cash equivalents, supplemented by cash generated
from operations, will be sufficient to fund operations (including increases in working capital required to fund any increases in activity
levels), scheduled debt retirement, any requirements under our Revolving Credit Facility to cash
collateralize letters of credit, and currently planned capital expenditures for 2011.
</DIV>


<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt"><!-- Folio -->40<!-- /Folio -->
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Cash Flows</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><B><I>Operating Activities </I></B>&#151; Net cash provided by our operating activities was $105.3&nbsp;million for the
year ended December&nbsp;31, 2010, compared to $62.9&nbsp;million for 2009. This increase in net cash
provided from operating activities was primarily attributable to a net decrease in the major
working capital components. The source of funds in 2009 was primarily attributable to the net
income from continuing operations, partially offset by higher working capital needs.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><B><I>Investing Activities </I></B><B>&#151; </B>Investing activities used $69.8&nbsp;million of net cash in 2010, compared to
$1.2&nbsp;million in 2009. During 2010, we used $177.1&nbsp;million to purchase property and equipment,
primarily for payments related to the construction of two new derrick/pipelay vessels (the <B><I>Global
1200 </I></B>and <B><I>Global 1201</I></B>), due to the upgrade of our fleet. This use of cash was partially offset by
proceeds from the sale of assets of $52.2&nbsp;million, proceeds of the sale of marketable securities of
$41.4&nbsp;million, and advance deposits received on the sale of assets of $16.8&nbsp;million. The net cash
used in 2009 was primarily due to the $122.0&nbsp;million purchase of property and equipment, primarily
for payments for the construction of the <B><I>Global 1200 </I></B>and <B><I>Global 1201</I></B>. Partially offsetting this
use of cash were proceeds from asset sales of $26.9&nbsp;million and a $93.4&nbsp;million decrease in our
restricted cash requirements due to the ending of our interim cash collateralization period under
our Revolving Credit Facility on June&nbsp;30, 2009. For information about the current status of our
cash collateralization under our Revolving Credit Facility, see the Liquidity Risk section below
and Note 9 of the Notes to Consolidated Financial Statements.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><B><I>Financing Activities </I></B>&#151; Financing activities used $31.4&nbsp;million of net cash in 2010 compared to
$4.5&nbsp;million in 2009. In 2010, we used $26.0&nbsp;million to pay long-term payables related to the
purchase of property and equipment. The net cash used in 2009 was primarily due to the repayment
of long-term debt.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Long-Term Debt</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Our long-term debt outstanding as of December&nbsp;31, 2010 and 2009 includes carrying amounts of $245.9
million and $236.9&nbsp;million, respectively, for the $325.0&nbsp;million principal amount of 2.75% Senior
Convertible Debentures which carry an interest rate of 2.75% per annum with semi-annual interest
payments. These debentures are convertible into cash, and potentially, into shares of our common
stock. We may redeem all or a part of the debentures any time on or after August&nbsp;1, 2014, for cash
at a price equal to 100% of the principal amount of the debentures being redeemed plus accrued and
unpaid interest. The holders of the debentures may require us to repurchase all or a part of their
debentures for cash on August&nbsp;1, 2017 and August&nbsp;1, 2022 at a price equal to 100% of the principal
amount of the debentures being redeemed plus accrued or unpaid interest, or upon the occurrence of
certain types of fundamental changes. Our 2.75% Senior Convertible Debentures contain a default
provision which permits the trustee or holders of the convertible debentures to accelerate such
indebtedness in the event of our failure to pay principal when due or upon a default that results
in the acceleration of any of our indebtedness in excess of $50&nbsp;million. For more information, see
Note 9 of the Notes to Consolidated Financial Statements.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">We also maintain $57.4&nbsp;million principal amount of Title XI bonds outstanding, which carry an
interest rate of 7.71% per annum with semi-annual principal payments of approximately $2.0&nbsp;million
payable each February and August until maturity in 2025. Our Title XI bonds contain a cross
default provision which provides that a default of our Revolving Credit Facility is a default under
our Title XI bonds which may result in our bonds becoming due and payable under certain
circumstances.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Our Revolving Credit Facility provides a borrowing capacity of up to $150.0&nbsp;million. As of
December&nbsp;31, 2010, we had no borrowings against the facility and $44.8&nbsp;million of letters of credit
outstanding thereunder. Due to the sale of vessels mortgaged under the Revolving Credit Facility, the
effective borrowing capacity under this facility at December&nbsp;31, 2010 is $134.1&nbsp;million, with
credit availability of $89.3&nbsp;million. We do have the option of increasing the capacity under this
facility to $250.0&nbsp;million by mortgaging one or more of our vessels that are not currently in the
collateralized vessel pool.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">As a result of our operating performance, we failed certain financial covenants of our Revolving
Credit Facility in the second, third, and fourth quarter of 2010. We amended the Revolving Credit
Facility in
</DIV>


<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt"><!-- Folio -->41<!-- /Folio -->
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always"><A HREF="#H79937tocpage">Table of Contents</A></H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">




<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">the second quarter of 2010 and received a waiver of compliance with the covenants for
the third quarter from the financial institutions participating in the Revolving Credit Facility.
As a result of the volatility in earnings associated with our business, effective as of February&nbsp;24,
2011, we amended our Revolving Credit Facility to permanently address this volatility and
cyclicality of our business by inserting a clause that allows us, at our option, to choose to cash
collateralize our letter of credit exposure, when covenant compliance, as defined in the Revolving
Credit Facility, is not possible and thereby achieve compliance. In addition, the amendment
includes a waiver of compliance with the covenant conditions for the fourth quarter of 2010.
During periods of cash collateralization, no borrowings, letters of credit, or bank guarantees
unsecured by cash are permitted. For additional discussion, see below under Liquidity Risk and
Note 9 of the Notes to Consolidated Financial Statement.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Our Revolving Credit Facility has a customary cross default provision triggered by a default of any
of our other indebtedness, the aggregate principal amount of which is in excess of $5&nbsp;million.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Other Indebtedness and Obligations</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">We also have a $6.0&nbsp;million short-term credit facility at one of our foreign locations, which is
secured by a letter of credit issued under our Revolving Credit Facility. At December&nbsp;31, 2010, we
had $1.6&nbsp;million of letters of credit outstanding and $4.4&nbsp;million of credit availability under
this particular credit facility.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><B><I>Charters </I></B><I>&#151; </I>We have a long-term charter for the <B><I>Titan 2</I></B>, a 408-foot self-propelled twin-hulled DP
derrick ship. The vessel charter payments are approximately $6.4&nbsp;million annually. The charter
term expires in May&nbsp;2018. This charter can be canceled by us at anytime, subject to a termination
penalty of the transfer to the vessel owner of title to our dynamic positioning (&#147;DP&#148;) system used
on the vessel. The DP system was purchased and installed on the <B><I>Titan 2</I></B>, at our cost, during the
first quarter of 2002 for a total cost of $8.9&nbsp;million, with a book value at December&nbsp;31, 2010 of
$1.1&nbsp;million. In February&nbsp;2011, we gave notice to the owner of the <B><I>Titan 2 </I></B>that we were
terminating the charter, effective in the second quarter of 2011. Consequently, we recorded a $7.9
million impairment of the leasehold improvements and deferred dry-dock costs related thereto.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">We also have a long-term charter for an MSV, the <B><I>Normand Commander. </I></B>The charter includes a
fixed-term five year lease with five annual renewal options, and requires monthly payments
denominated in Norwegian kroners at an annual rate of approximately 69.4&nbsp;million kroners (or $11.8
million as of December&nbsp;31, 2010). As of December&nbsp;31, 2010, we had entered into forward foreign
currency contracts that have enabled us to fulfill 31.5&nbsp;million of our remaining non-cancellable
Norwegian kroner obligations under this charter at an average rate of 6.23 kroners per U.S. dollar.
In February&nbsp;2011, we gave notice of our intent to renew the charter of this vessel for one year
ending June&nbsp;2012.
</DIV>
